Um, alongside the high energy? I don't think it was at the exact same time. But lately... lately I just wake up in the temporary apartment with this really heavy, hollow feeling. It's like the day is already ruined before I even drive to the plant. 

Which, honestly, being in a temporary apartment is so disorienting and probably doesn't help. I had to move in there last month because a pipe burst in my actual place. Water literally everywhere. The plumber they sent out was this guy who just wouldn't stop talking about his fantasy football team while he was supposed to be cutting drywall. I don't even watch football. My dad used to, but he was a huge Cubs fan, so baseball was really more our thing. We used to go to games when I was a kid and eat those terrible stadium hot dogs. The ones wrapped in foil? 

But anyway, the water damage completely ruined my favorite rug, so I'm stuck in this weird furnished place with terrible corporate art on the walls, and... wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? The projects?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, sleep... sleep was completely different then. I was only getting maybe two or three hours a night. But the weird thing was, I'd still show up at the plant the next morning feeling totally sharp and charged. It honestly felt like sleep was just optional, you know? Like it was a really inefficient use of my time when I had so much to do. 

Which is funny because usually I am someone who needs my full eight hours. I bought this really expensive mattress last year, one of those memory foam ones that's supposed to cool you down while you sleep? Because I always run hot at night. My sister is the exact opposite, she sleeps with three blankets even in the summer. She lives in Arizona, too, which makes absolutely no sense. I went to visit her once in July and I thought I was going to melt. We went to this botanical garden there and saw all these crazy cacti. Did you know some cacti only bloom at night? They have these huge white flowers that open in the dark so bats can pollinate them. I watched a whole video on it when I was awake at 3 AM during that project phase. 

But anyway, yeah... the mattress got ruined in the flood, unfortunately. The water just soaked right into the foam. So now I'm sleeping on this terrible spring mattress at the temporary place. But back when I was doing all those projects, it didn't even matter what I slept on because I barely used the bed anyway. I just didn't need it.

Um, how many days? I'm not entirely sure, honestly. It's kind of a blur. I mean, I know it was a while, but I didn't exactly have a calendar going, marking off the days with a big red X, you know? 

Speaking of calendars, I used to buy those really beautiful wall calendars every year. The ones with the Ansel Adams photographs? Black and white landscapes. They just looked so classy in my old office before the flood. I had this one picture of Yosemite that was just breathtaking. Have you ever been to Yosemite? I was supposed to go on a hiking trip there with my college roommate a few years ago, but she ended up twisting her ankle like two days before we were supposed to fly out. She stepped off a curb wrong while she was texting. It's crazy how something so small can just ruin an entire vacation. We ended up having to cancel the flights, and getting a refund from that airline was an absolute nightmare. I spent literally four hours on hold listening to this awful elevator music. It was that one song, "The Girl from Ipanema," just playing on a loop. To this day, if I hear that song in a grocery store, my eye starts twitching. 

But yeah, the days... I don't know the exact number of days. It was just a chunk of time where I felt really good and didn't need to sleep.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Seven days? Um... I really couldn't tell you for sure if it was a full week or not. Time just sort of flowed differently then. I wasn't keeping track of it like I do at work. 

You know, my production schedules at the plant are always strictly broken down into seven-day cycles. We manufacture these specialized industrial valves, and if the timeline gets thrown off by even half a shift, the whole week is basically ruined. Actually, we had this massive delay right before the flood because our main supplier sent the wrong grade of steel. It was supposed to be stainless, but they sent galvanized. You absolutely cannot use galvanized for high-pressure systems, it's a complete disaster waiting to happen. I had to get on the phone with the vendor, and their rep—this guy named Todd—actually tried to tell me it was my fault for not checking the shipping manifest the second the truck arrived. Can you believe the nerve? Todd is always pulling stuff like that. Plus he wears these really loud novelty ties to our meetings, like with Looney Tunes characters on them. It is so wildly unprofessional. I think someone finally complained to his regional manager about it, because last time I saw him, he was just wearing a plain navy one. He looked so miserable. 

But um, what was the question? Oh, the seven days. Yeah, I honestly don't know if the energy lasted exactly a week or if it was shorter. I just wasn't counting the days.

Four days? Um... yeah, I guess it could have been four days. Maybe more? I really, honestly couldn't tell you. It's just hard to pinpoint exactly when it started and stopped. 

Kind of like when my car started making this weird rattling noise last month. I couldn't tell exactly when it began, you know? It just sort of snuck up on me. I thought it was just a loose license plate or something, but it turned out the whole exhaust pipe was rusting through. I had to take it to this mechanic my dad recommended, this guy named Sal. Sal is a character, let me tell you. He has this massive German Shepherd that just wanders around the garage while he's working. The dog's name is Brutus, and he is the sweetest thing, but he gets grease all over everything. I was wearing my good beige slacks—the ones I bought for that conference in Denver—and Brutus came right up and leaned against my leg. Just a giant black grease stain on the side of my knee. I tried everything to get it out, club soda, dish soap, even that expensive stain remover you see on infomercials, but it was completely ruined. I ended up having to throw them away, which was a shame because they had real pockets. You know how hard it is to find women's pants with actual, functional pockets? It's a conspiracy, I swear. The handbag industry is definitely behind it. 

But anyway... four days? Sure, I suppose it could have been at least four days. I just really wasn't keeping track.

Did anyone say anything? Um, I don't really know. I don't think anyone explicitly sat me down and said, "Wow, you're not yourself." I mean, maybe they noticed I was getting more done? I was definitely clearing out my inbox faster than usual. 

Speaking of inboxes, the new email system they rolled out at the plant is an absolute nightmare. They have these huge white flowers that open in the dark so bats can pollinate them. I watched a whole video on it when I was awake at 3 AM during that project phase. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... the mattress got ruined in the flood, unfortunately. The water just soaked right into the foam. So now I'm sleeping on this terrible spring mattress at the temporary place. But back when I was doing all those projects, it didn't even matter what I slept on because I barely used the bed anyway. I just didn't need it.\n\nDoctor: During these past three months of feeling heavy and hollow, have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: Um, hurting myself? No, no, I haven't had any thoughts like that. It's just... it's just this really heavy feeling, like I said. But no, nothing about not wanting to be here. \n\nThough I will say, being in that temporary apartment makes me feel like I'm not really anywhere, you know? It's so sterile. I was trying to make it feel a little more homey last weekend, so I went to this thrift store down the street. I love thrift stores. You never know what you're going to find. I actually found this amazing vintage Pyrex bowl—the green one with the little flowers on it? My grandmother used to have the exact same one, and she would always make this incredible potato salad for family picnics. She put radishes in it, which sounds weird, but it gave it this really great crunch. I tried making it once for a potluck at the plant, but I couldn't get the dressing right. I think she used a specific brand of mayonnaise that they don't make anymore. \n\nAnyway, I bought the bowl, but then I realized I don't even have a mixer at the temporary place, so it's just sitting on the counter right now. But yeah... no thoughts of hurting myself. Just the heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: During that period of high energy and barely sleeping, did the intensity of your activity ever get so extreme that it caused serious problems—like conflicts at work, risky decisions, or anyone suggesting you needed to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Um, no, nothing like that. I mean, I was getting a ton done, so if anything, people at work were probably just wondering how I was managing it all. But no one said I needed to go to a hospital or anything crazy like that. \n\nThough speaking of hospitals, did I tell you about the time my cousin had to go to the ER because she swallowed a quarter? We were like eight, and she was trying to do this magic trick she saw on TV. The magician made a coin disappear, but she just completely swallowed it. My aunt was freaking out, driving like a maniac to the hospital. And the funny thing is, the doctor just told them to wait for it to... you know, pass. They didn't even have to do surgery. She still has the quarter, actually. She keeps it in this little glass jar on her desk at work. She's an accountant now, which is hilarious when you think about it—working with money all day. She just got promoted to senior auditor at this big firm in Chicago. I've always wanted to visit Chicago, mostly for the deep-dish pizza. I saw this travel show where they went to the original place that invented it, and the cheese pull was just insane. It was like a foot long. \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Just working a lot.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did that period of high energy, needing very little sleep, and rapid activity last?\n\nPatient: Um, how many days? I'm not entirely sure, honestly. It's kind of a blur. I mean, I know it was a while, but I didn't exactly have a calendar going, marking off the days with a big red X, you know? \n\nSpeaking of calendars, I used to buy those really beautiful wall calendars every year. The ones with the Ansel Adams photographs? Black and white landscapes. They just looked so classy in my old office before the flood. I had this one picture of Yosemite that was just breathtaking. Have you ever been to Yosemite? I was supposed to go on a hiking trip there with my college roommate a few years ago, but she ended up twisting her ankle like two days before we were supposed to fly out. She stepped off a curb wrong while she was texting. It's crazy how something so small can just ruin an entire vacation. We ended up having to cancel the flights, and getting a refund from that airline was an absolute nightmare. I spent literally four hours on hold listening to this awful elevator music. It was that one song, \"The Girl from Ipanema,\" just playing on a loop. To this day, if I hear that song in a grocery store, my eye starts twitching. \n\nBut yeah, the days... I don't know the exact number of days. It was just a chunk of time where I felt really good and didn't need to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back on that period of high energy and barely sleeping, did it last for at least a full week—seven days or more in a row—or was it shorter than that?\n\nPatient: Seven days? I was wearing my good beige slacks—the ones I bought for that conference in Denver—and Brutus came right up and leaned against my leg. Just a giant black grease stain on the side of my knee. I tried everything to get it out, club soda, dish soap, even that expensive stain remover you see on infomercials, but it was completely ruined. I ended up having to throw them away, which was a shame because they had real pockets. You know how hard it is to find women's pants with actual, functional pockets? It's a conspiracy, I swear. The handbag industry is definitely behind it. \n\nBut anyway... four days? Sure, I suppose it could have been at least four days. I just really wasn't keeping track.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy, low-sleep period, did anyone close to you—coworkers, friends, or family—comment that you seemed noticeably different from your usual self, like unusually upbeat, talkative, or 'not yourself'?\n\nPatient: Did anyone say anything? Problems? Well... yeah, I guess it did cause some issues at the plant. I actually had to ask to be pulled out of a shift-planning meeting. I just couldn't carry out the basic production decisions that my whole role depends on, my brain just wasn't doing it. It got so messy that occupational health had to get involved.

The nurse, Diane, sits at her desk with this tiny space heater pointed directly at her feet. I am pretty sure those space heaters are a massive fire hazard, especially in an industrial facility. We had a guy in the warehouse, Gary, who brought one in last winter and it tripped the breaker for the entire packaging line. We lost like three hours of work because maintenance had to come reset everything, and Gary tried to blame it on the microwave in the breakroom. But everyone knows nobody uses that microwave because someone burned tilapia in it back in 2019 and the smell never fully left. You really can never get burnt fish smell out of plastic, it just seeps into the pores or something. My sister tried to tell me baking soda works for that, but she also thinks crystals can cure seasonal allergies, so I take her advice with a grain of salt. She has this giant amethyst on her nightstand that she swears clears her sinuses... 

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, if things were falling apart. Yeah, so occupational health stepped in because I just couldn't make those basic decisions to do my job properly during that time.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.81, F31.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—thanks for coming. To start off, can you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Yeah, thanks. Um, I guess I came in because... well, work has been really intense. I manage industrial production at this big plant, and a little while ago I was just launching these nonstop redesign projects. Which, actually, getting the permits for that is such a headache. The city council takes forever. My uncle used to work for the city, actually, in zoning, and he always said the bureaucracy is just designed to slow you down. He collects vintage radios now, which is kind of a weird hobby, they take up his whole garage. But anyway. \n\nWhere was I? Right, the plant. So I was doing all that, calling our suppliers way after hours, completely rewriting the shift plan by myself. Because I have my master's degree in this, so I know how to optimize the floor, but... wait, what was the question? Oh, why I'm here. Yeah. It's just... after doing all of that, suddenly I just couldn't keep up with the very plans I had set in motion. Like, at all.\n\nDoctor: During that same time when you were taking on all these extra projects and couldn't slow down, did you also notice feelings of sadness, hopelessness, or emptiness alongside the high energy?\n\nPatient: Um, alongside the high energy? I don't think it was at the exact same time. But lately... lately I just wake up in the temporary apartment with this really heavy, hollow feeling. It's like the day is already ruined before I even drive to the plant. \n\nWhich, honestly, being in a temporary apartment is so disorienting and probably doesn't help. I had to move in there last month because a pipe burst in my actual place. Water literally everywhere. The plumber they sent out was this guy who just wouldn't stop talking about his fantasy football team while he was supposed to be cutting drywall. I don't even watch football. My dad used to, but he was a huge Cubs fan, so baseball was really more our thing. We used to go to games when I was a kid and eat those terrible stadium hot dogs. The ones wrapped in foil? \n\nBut anyway, the water damage completely ruined my favorite rug, so I'm stuck in this weird furnished place with terrible corporate art on the walls, and... wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? The projects?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hollow feeling in the mornings—days, weeks?\n\nPatient: Oh, how long? Um, I'd say the really heavy stretch has been going on for about three months now. Yeah, three months. Which, you know, getting this temporary plant assignment right in the middle of it and then having to move... it's just made everything so much harder to manage. \n\nAnd moving is the worst anyway, isn't it? I swear, I have so many boxes that I haven't unpacked because what's the point if I'm going back to my real apartment eventually? I bought these special plastic bins that are supposed to be waterproof, just in case, because after the pipe bursting, I'm completely paranoid about water damage. I saw this documentary once about mold in houses and how it can cause all these crazy health problems. Like, people having memory loss and fatigue just from breathing in spores. It was narrated by that actor... oh, what's his name? The one who was in that movie with the aliens? \n\nAnyway, I keep checking the corners of the temporary apartment for mold now. It smells a little musty, but maybe that's just because the carpet is old. They really should update the flooring in those corporate rentals. But yeah, to answer your question... wait, you asked about the mornings, right? Yeah, about three months of waking up feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you were taking on all those extra projects and working after hours, did you notice you needed much less sleep than usual, but still felt full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep... sleep was completely different then. I was only getting maybe two or three hours a night. But the weird thing was, I'd still show up at the plant the next morning feeling totally sharp and charged. It honestly felt like sleep was just optional, you know? Like it was a really inefficient use of my time when I had so much to do. \n\nWhich is funny because usually I am someone who needs my full eight hours. I bought this really expensive mattress last year, one of those memory foam ones that's supposed to cool you down while you sleep? Because I always run hot at night. My sister is the exact opposite, she sleeps with three blankets even in the summer. She lives in Arizona, too, which makes absolutely no sense. I went to visit her once in July and I thought I was going to melt. We went to this botanical garden there and saw all these crazy cacti. Did you know some cacti only bloom at night? They have these huge white flowers that open in the dark so bats can pollinate them. I watched a whole video on it when I was awake at 3 AM during that project phase. \n\nBut anyway, yeah... the mattress got ruined in the flood, unfortunately. The water just soaked right into the foam. So now I'm sleeping on this terrible spring mattress at the temporary place. But back when I was doing all those projects, it didn't even matter what I slept on because I barely used the bed anyway. I just didn't need it.\n\nDoctor: During these past three months of feeling heavy and hollow, have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: Um, hurting myself? No, no, I haven't had any thoughts like that. It's just... it's just this really heavy feeling, like I said. But no, nothing about not wanting to be here. \n\nThough I will say, being in that temporary apartment makes me feel like I'm not really anywhere, you know? It's so sterile. I was trying to make it feel a little more homey last weekend, so I went to this thrift store down the street. I love thrift stores. You never know what you're going to find. I actually found this amazing vintage Pyrex bowl—the green one with the little flowers on it? My grandmother used to have the exact same one, and she would always make this incredible potato salad for family picnics. She put radishes in it, which sounds weird, but it gave it this really great crunch. I tried making it once for a potluck at the plant, but I couldn't get the dressing right. I think she used a specific brand of mayonnaise that they don't make anymore. \n\nAnyway, I bought the bowl, but then I realized I don't even have a mixer at the temporary place, so it's just sitting on the counter right now. But yeah... no thoughts of hurting myself. Just the heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: During that period of high energy and barely sleeping, did the intensity of your activity ever get so extreme that it caused serious problems—like conflicts at work, risky decisions, or anyone suggesting you needed to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Um, no, nothing like that. I mean, I was getting a ton done, so if anything, people at work were probably just wondering how I was managing it all. But no one said I needed to go to a hospital or anything crazy like that. \n\nThough speaking of hospitals, did I tell you about the time my cousin had to go to the ER because she swallowed a quarter? We were like eight, and she was trying to do this magic trick she saw on TV. The magician made a coin disappear, but she just completely swallowed it. My aunt was freaking out, driving like a maniac to the hospital. And the funny thing is, the doctor just told them to wait for it to... you know, pass. They didn't even have to do surgery. She still has the quarter, actually. She keeps it in this little glass jar on her desk at work. She's an accountant now, which is hilarious when you think about it—working with money all day. She just got promoted to senior auditor at this big firm in Chicago. I've always wanted to visit Chicago, mostly for the deep-dish pizza. I saw this travel show where they went to the original place that invented it, and the cheese pull was just insane. It was like a foot long. \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the high energy stuff. Yeah, no serious problems or hospital visits or anything. Just working a lot.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did that period of high energy, needing very little sleep, and rapid activity last?\n\nPatient: Um, how many days? I'm not entirely sure, honestly. It's kind of a blur. I mean, I know it was a while, but I didn't exactly have a calendar going, marking off the days with a big red X, you know? \n\nSpeaking of calendars, I used to buy those really beautiful wall calendars every year. The ones with the Ansel Adams photographs? Black and white landscapes. They just looked so classy in my old office before the flood. I had this one picture of Yosemite that was just breathtaking. Have you ever been to Yosemite? I was supposed to go on a hiking trip there with my college roommate a few years ago, but she ended up twisting her ankle like two days before we were supposed to fly out. She stepped off a curb wrong while she was texting. It's crazy how something so small can just ruin an entire vacation. We ended up having to cancel the flights, and getting a refund from that airline was an absolute nightmare. I spent literally four hours on hold listening to this awful elevator music. It was that one song, \"The Girl from Ipanema,\" just playing on a loop. To this day, if I hear that song in a grocery store, my eye starts twitching. \n\nBut yeah, the days... I don't know the exact number of days. It was just a chunk of time where I felt really good and didn't need to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back on that period of high energy and barely sleeping, did it last for at least a full week—seven days or more in a row—or was it shorter than that?\n\nPatient: Seven days? Um... I really couldn't tell you for sure if it was a full week or not. Time just sort of flowed differently then. I wasn't keeping track of it like I do at work. \n\nYou know, my production schedules at the plant are always strictly broken down into seven-day cycles. We manufacture these specialized industrial valves, and if the timeline gets thrown off by even half a shift, the whole week is basically ruined. Actually, we had this massive delay right before the flood because our main supplier sent the wrong grade of steel. It was supposed to be stainless, but they sent galvanized. You absolutely cannot use galvanized for high-pressure systems, it's a complete disaster waiting to happen. I had to get on the phone with the vendor, and their rep—this guy named Todd—actually tried to tell me it was my fault for not checking the shipping manifest the second the truck arrived. Can you believe the nerve? Todd is always pulling stuff like that. Plus he wears these really loud novelty ties to our meetings, like with Looney Tunes characters on them. It is so wildly unprofessional. I think someone finally complained to his regional manager about it, because last time I saw him, he was just wearing a plain navy one. He looked so miserable. \n\nBut um, what was the question? Oh, the seven days. Yeah, I honestly don't know if the energy lasted exactly a week or if it was shorter. I just wasn't counting the days.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're not sure it was a full week, do you think that high-energy, barely-sleeping stretch lasted at least four days in a row?\n\nPatient: Four days? Um... yeah, I guess it could have been four days. Maybe more? I really, honestly couldn't tell you. It's just hard to pinpoint exactly when it started and stopped. \n\nKind of like when my car started making this weird rattling noise last month. I couldn't tell exactly when it began, you know? It just sort of snuck up on me. I thought it was just a loose license plate or something, but it turned out the whole exhaust pipe was rusting through. I had to take it to this mechanic my dad recommended, this guy named Sal. Sal is a character, let me tell you. He has this massive German Shepherd that just wanders around the garage while he's working. The dog's name is Brutus, and he is the sweetest thing, but he gets grease all over everything. I was wearing my good beige slacks—the ones I bought for that conference in Denver—and Brutus came right up and leaned against my leg. Just a giant black grease stain on the side of my knee. I tried everything to get it out, club soda, dish soap, even that expensive stain remover you see on infomercials, but it was completely ruined. I ended up having to throw them away, which was a shame because they had real pockets. You know how hard it is to find women's pants with actual, functional pockets? It's a conspiracy, I swear. The handbag industry is definitely behind it. \n\nBut anyway... four days? Sure, I suppose it could have been at least four days. I just really wasn't keeping track.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy, low-sleep period, did anyone close to you—coworkers, friends, or family—comment that you seemed noticeably different from your usual self, like unusually upbeat, talkative, or 'not yourself'?\n\nPatient: Did anyone say anything? Um, I don't really know. I don't think anyone explicitly sat me down and said, \"Wow, you're not yourself.\" I mean, maybe they noticed I was getting more done? I was definitely clearing out my inbox faster than usual. \n\nSpeaking of inboxes, the new email system they rolled out at the plant is an absolute nightmare. They switched us over to this cloud-based thing over the weekend without warning anyone, and on Monday morning, half the floor couldn't even log in. It was chaos. My assistant, Brenda, she was practically in tears because she couldn't access the shipping manifests for the morning freight. Brenda gets stressed out so easily. I keep telling her she needs to try yoga or maybe just switch to decaf, but she drinks those massive iced coffees from the place down the street. The ones with the caramel drizzle that are basically just milkshakes? I don't know how she drinks those every single day and doesn't bounce off the walls. I had one once, just a small one, and I felt like my heart was going to beat right out of my chest. I had to go walk around the block three times just to burn off the sugar. And the line at that coffee shop is always out the door, rain or shine. People will literally stand in a blizzard for a five-dollar latte, it's wild. \n\nBut um, no, I don't really remember anyone making a big deal about how I was acting. If they did, I probably just brushed it off.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did the way you were acting cause any real problems in your relationships or day-to-day responsibilities—like arguments with family, financial risks, or things falling apart because of how intensely you were working?\n\nPatient: Problems? Well... yeah, I guess it did cause some issues at the plant. I actually had to ask to be pulled out of a shift-planning meeting. I just couldn't carry out the basic production decisions that my whole role depends on, my brain just wasn't doing it. It got so messy that occupational health had to get involved. \n\nWhich, by the way, the occupational health office is down on the first floor near the loading dock, and it is always freezing in there. The nurse, Diane, sits at her desk with this tiny space heater pointed directly at her feet. I am pretty sure those space heaters are a massive fire hazard, especially in an industrial facility. We had a guy in the warehouse, Gary, who brought one in last winter and it tripped the breaker for the entire packaging line. We lost like three hours of work because maintenance had to come reset everything, and Gary tried to blame it on the microwave in the breakroom. But everyone knows nobody uses that microwave because someone burned tilapia in it back in 2019 and the smell never fully left. You really can never get burnt fish smell out of plastic, it just seeps into the pores or something. My sister tried to tell me baking soda works for that, but she also thinks crystals can cure seasonal allergies, so I take her advice with a grain of salt. She has this giant amethyst on her nightstand that she swears clears her sinuses... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, if things were falling apart. Yeah, so occupational health stepped in because I just couldn't make those basic decisions to do my job properly during that time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F31.3", "F31.4"], "note": "The description of clear functional impairment during the high-energy period (inability to perform basic job duties, occupational health intervention) indicates true manic-level impairment rather than hypomania, ruling out Bipolar II (F31.81) in favor of Bipolar I with current depressive episode. Given the persistent, moderately impairing depressive symptoms without psychosis or suicidality, both moderate (F31.3) and severe without psychotic features (F31.4) remain plausible pending further symptom detail.", "is_final": false}

Yeah, about three months of waking up feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you were taking on all those extra projects and working after hours, did you notice you needed much less sleep than usual, but still felt full of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep... sleep was completely different then. It honestly felt like sleep was just optional, you know? Like it was a really inefficient use of my time when I had so much to do. \n\nWhich is funny because usually I am someone who needs my full eight hours. I bought this really expensive mattress last year, one of those memory foam ones that's supposed to cool you down while you sleep? Because I always run hot at night. My sister is the exact opposite, she sleeps with three blankets even in the summer. She lives in Arizona, too, which makes absolutely no sense. I went to visit her once in July and I thought I was going to melt. We went to this botanical garden there and saw all these crazy cacti. Did you know some cacti only bloom at night? I actually found this amazing vintage Pyrex bowl—the green one with the little flowers on it? My grandmother used to have the exact same one, and she would always make this incredible potato salad for family picnics. She put radishes in it, which sounds weird, but it gave it this really great crunch. I tried making it once for a potluck at the plant, but I couldn't get the dressing right. I think she used a specific brand of mayonnaise that they don't make anymore. \n\nAnyway, I bought the bowl, but then I realized I don't even have a mixer at the temporary place, so it's just sitting on the counter right now. But yeah... no thoughts of hurting myself. Just the heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: During that period of high energy and barely sleeping, did the intensity of your activity ever get so extreme that it caused serious problems—like conflicts at work, risky decisions, or anyone suggesting you needed to be hospitalized?\n\nPatient: Um, no, nothing like that. I mean, I was getting a ton done, so if anything, people at work were probably just wondering how I was managing it all. But no one said I needed to go to a hospital or anything crazy like that. \n\nThough speaking of hospitals, did I tell you about the time my cousin had to go to the ER because she swallowed a quarter? We were like eight, and she was trying to do this magic trick she saw on TV. The magician made a coin disappear, but she just completely swallowed it. My aunt was freaking out, driving like a maniac to the hospital. And the funny thing is, the doctor just told them to wait for it to... you know, pass. They didn't even have to do surgery. She still has the quarter, actually. She keeps it in this little glass jar on her desk at work. She's an accountant now, which is hilarious when you think about it—working with money all day. She just got promoted to senior auditor at this big firm in Chicago. I've always wanted to visit Chicago, mostly for the deep-dish pizza. I saw this travel show where they went to the original place that invented it, and the cheese pull was just insane. It was like a foot long. \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the high energy stuff. Yeah, no serious problems or hospital visits or anything. Just working a lot.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did that period of high energy, needing very little sleep, and rapid activity last?\n\nPatient: Um, how many days? I'm not entirely sure, honestly. It's kind of a blur. I mean, I know it was a while, but I didn't exactly have a calendar going, marking off the days with a big red X, you know? \n\nSpeaking of calendars, I used to buy those really beautiful wall calendars every year. The ones with the Ansel Adams photographs? Black and white landscapes. They just looked so classy in my old office before the flood. I had this one picture of Yosemite that was just breathtaking. Have you ever been to Yosemite? I was supposed to go on a hiking trip there with my college roommate a few years ago, but she ended up twisting her ankle like two days before we were supposed to fly out. She stepped off a curb wrong while she was texting. It's crazy how something so small can just ruin an entire vacation. We ended up having to cancel the flights, and getting a refund from that airline was an absolute nightmare. I spent literally four hours on hold listening to this awful elevator music. It was that one song, \"The Girl from Ipanema,\" just playing on a loop. To this day, if I hear that song in a grocery store, my eye starts twitching. \n\nBut yeah, the days... I don't know the exact number of days. It was just a chunk of time where I felt really good and didn't need to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back on that period of high energy and barely sleeping, did it last for at least a full week—seven days or more in a row—or was it shorter than that?\n\nPatient: Seven days? Um... I really couldn't tell you for sure if it was a full week or not. Time just sort of flowed differently then. I wasn't keeping track of it like I do at work. \n\nYou know, my production schedules at the plant are always strictly broken down into seven-day cycles. We manufacture these specialized industrial valves, and if the timeline gets thrown off by even half a shift, the whole week is basically ruined. Actually, we had this massive delay right before the flood because our main supplier sent the wrong grade of steel. It was supposed to be stainless, but they sent galvanized. You absolutely cannot use galvanized for high-pressure systems, it's a complete disaster waiting to happen. I had to get on the phone with the vendor, and their rep—this guy named Todd—actually tried to tell me it was my fault for not checking the shipping manifest the second the truck arrived. Can you believe the nerve? Todd is always pulling stuff like that. Plus he wears these really loud novelty ties to our meetings, like with Looney Tunes characters on them. It is so wildly unprofessional. I think someone finally complained to his regional manager about it, because last time I saw him, he was just wearing a plain navy one. He looked so miserable. \n\nBut um, what was the question? Oh, the seven days. Yeah, I honestly don't know if the energy lasted exactly a week or if it was shorter. I just wasn't counting the days.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're not sure it was a full week, do you think that high-energy, barely-sleeping stretch lasted at least four days in a row?\n\nPatient: Four days? Um... yeah, I guess it could have been four days. Maybe more? I really, honestly couldn't tell you. It's just hard to pinpoint exactly when it started and stopped. \n\nKind of like when my car started making this weird rattling noise last month. I couldn't tell exactly when it began, you know? It just sort of snuck up on me. I thought it was just a loose license plate or something, but it turned out the whole exhaust pipe was rusting through. I had to take it to this mechanic my dad recommended, this guy named Sal. Sal is a character, let me tell you. He has this massive German Shepherd that just wanders around the garage while he's working. The dog's name is Brutus, and he is the sweetest thing, but he gets grease all over everything. I was wearing my good beige slacks—the ones I bought for that conference in Denver—and Brutus came right up and leaned against my leg. Just a giant black grease stain on the side of my knee. I tried everything to get it out, club soda, dish soap, even that expensive stain remover you see on infomercials, but it was completely ruined. I ended up having to throw them away, which was a shame because they had real pockets. You know how hard it is to find women's pants with actual, functional pockets? It's a conspiracy, I swear. The handbag industry is definitely behind it. \n\nBut anyway... four days? Sure, I suppose it could have been at least four days. I just really wasn't keeping track.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy, low-sleep period, did anyone close to you—coworkers, friends, or family—comment that you seemed noticeably different from your usual self, like unusually upbeat, talkative, or 'not yourself'?\n\nPatient: Did anyone say anything? Um, I don't really know. I don't think anyone explicitly sat me down and said, \"Wow, you're not yourself.\" I mean, maybe they noticed I was getting more done? I was definitely clearing out my inbox faster than usual. \n\nSpeaking of inboxes, the new email system they rolled out at the plant is an absolute nightmare. They switched us over to this cloud-based thing over the weekend without warning anyone, and on Monday morning, half the floor couldn't even log in. It was chaos. My assistant, Brenda, she was practically in tears because she couldn't access the shipping manifests for the morning freight. Brenda gets stressed out so easily. I keep telling her she needs to try yoga or maybe just switch to decaf, but she drinks those massive iced coffees from the place down the street. The ones with the caramel drizzle that are basically just milkshakes? I don't know how she drinks those every single day and doesn't bounce off the walls. I had one once, just a small one, and I felt like my heart was going to beat right out of my chest. I had to go walk around the block three times just to burn off the sugar. And the line at that coffee shop is always out the door, rain or shine. People will literally stand in a blizzard for a five-dollar latte, it's wild. \n\nBut um, no, I don't really remember anyone making a big deal about how I was acting. If they did, I probably just brushed it off.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did the way you were acting cause any real problems in your relationships or day-to-day responsibilities—like arguments with family, financial risks, or things falling apart because of how intensely you were working?\n\nPatient: Problems? Well... yeah, I guess it did cause some issues at the plant. I actually had to ask to be pulled out of a shift-planning meeting. I just couldn't carry out the basic production decisions that my whole role depends on, my brain just wasn't doing it. It got so messy that occupational health had to get involved. \n\nWhich, by the way, the occupational health office is down on the first floor near the loading dock, and it is always freezing in there. The nurse, Diane, sits at her desk with this tiny space heater pointed directly at her feet. I am pretty sure those space heaters are a massive fire hazard, especially in an industrial facility. We had a guy in the warehouse, Gary, who brought one in last winter and it tripped the breaker for the entire packaging line. We lost like three hours of work because maintenance had to come reset everything, and Gary tried to blame it on the microwave in the breakroom. But everyone knows nobody uses that microwave because someone burned tilapia in it back in 2019 and the smell never fully left. You really can never get burnt fish smell out of plastic, it just seeps into the pores or something. My sister tried to tell me baking soda works for that, but she also thinks crystals can cure seasonal allergies, so I take her advice with a grain of salt. She has this giant amethyst on her nightstand that she swears clears her sinuses... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, if things were falling apart. Yeah, so occupational health stepped in because I just couldn't make those basic decisions to do my job properly during that time.\n\nDoctor: During these past three months of feeling heavy and hollow, has this feeling made it hard for you to keep up with your daily responsibilities—like going to work, taking care of yourself, or managing basic tasks—or have you still been able to get through most of your day fairly normally?\n\nPatient: Hard to keep up? I remember my dad rented this awful maroon minivan that didn't have working air conditioning in the back, so my sister and I just baked the entire drive. We had to roll the windows down, but then the wind would just whip my hair directly into my eyes. I had these really tragic bangs back then. I cut them myself with kitchen scissors because I wanted to look like this girl from a magazine, but I ended up looking like a medieval peasant. I just can't make the decisions I need to make at work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Push through? No, no I haven't been able to just push through. Like I said, it got to the point where I had to actually ask to be pulled out of a shift-planning meeting. I just couldn't do it. I couldn't make the basic production decisions that my entire role depends on, so occupational health had to get involved. 

And honestly, going down to occupational health is a whole ordeal on its own because the elevator in that wing of the building has been making this horrible grinding noise for like three weeks. It sounds like metal chewing on metal. I keep telling the maintenance guys, especially Rick, that they need to get a specialist in to look at the cables, but Rick just sprays WD-40 on the doors and calls it a day. Rick is getting close to retirement, I think he's just coasting at this point. He told me he bought a pontoon boat that he's fixing up in his driveway. He wants to take it down to the lake and just fish for bass all day. My uncle had a pontoon boat when I was growing up, we used to take it out on the Fourth of July and watch the fireworks from the water. It was great until the year my cousin dropped his phone in the lake trying to take a picture of a duck. It was one of those old flip phones, so it was completely ruined. He was so mad because he had just bought this ridiculous ringtone for it... 

Um, but what were we talking about? Oh, right, work. Yeah, I haven't been able to just get by. I had to step out of that meeting and go to occupational health because I just couldn't make the decisions I needed to make.
